Phonon Self-Energy Corrections: To Screen, or Not to Screen
Jan Berges, Nina Girotto, Tim Wehling, Nicola Marzari, Samuel Poncé

Abstract
First-principles calculations of phonons are often based on the adiabatic approximation and on Brillouin-zone samplings that might not always be sufficient to capture the subtleties of Kohn anomalies. These shortcomings can be addressed through corrections to the phonon self-energy arising from the low-energy electrons. The exact self-energy involves a product of a bare and a screened electron-phonon vertex [ Rev. Mod. Phys. 89 , 015003 (2017) ]; still, calculations often employ two adiabatically screened vertices, which have been proposed as a reliable approximation for self-energy differences [ Phys. Rev. B 82 , 165111 (2010) ]. We assess the accuracy of both approaches in estimating the phonon spectral functions of model Hamiltonians and the adiabatic low-temperature phonon dispersions of monolayer TaS 2 and doped MoS 2 . We find that the approximate method yields excellent corrections at low computational cost, due to its designed error cancellation to first order, while using a bare vertex could in principle improve these results but is challenging in practice. We offer an alternative strategy based on downfolding to partially screened phonons and interactions [ Phys. Rev. B 92 , 245108 (2015) ]. This is a natural scheme to include electron-electron interactions and tackle phonons in strongly correlated materials and the frequency dependence of the electron-phonon vertex.



I
Introduction

The interplay between interacting electrons and lattice vibrations in solids gives rise to diverse phenomena ranging from the quantitative, such as changes in the electric
Poncé et al. 2020
or thermal conductivities
Knoop et al. 2020
, to the qualitative, such as instabilities toward charge
Wilson et al. 1974
and superconducting order
Revolinsky et al. 1963
.
Accordingly, the simulation of lattice dynamics is an exceptionally well established branch of condensed-matter physics
Baroni et al. 2001
.
Readily accessible total energies from
DFT
(
DFT
)
Hohenberg and Kohn 1964
;
Kohn and Sham 1965
and the development of
DFPT
(
DFPT
)
Zein 1984
;
Gonze and Lee 1997
;
Baroni et al. 2001
have significantly advanced this field.
Nowadays, phonon frequencies and normal modes are obtained routinely and at reasonable computational cost, enabling even high-throughput studies
Petretto et al. 2018
;
Mounet et al. 2018
.
For a wide range of materials, from semiconductors
Baroni et al. 1987
;
Giannozzi et al. 1991
to metals
de Gironcoli 1995
, the agreement between theory and experiment is remarkable.

Nevertheless,
DFPT
still depends on crucial approximations:
First, the adiabatic or Born-Oppenheimer approximation
Born and Oppenheimer 1927
implicitly assumes that the dynamics of electrons and ions happens on two well separated energy scales.
However, in some materials the relevant energies of the electrons are similar to or even smaller than those of the phonons, leading to nonadiabatic effects
Maksimov and Shulga 1996
;
Lazzeri and Mauri 2006
;
Pisana et al. 2007
;
Bauer and Falter 2009
;
Calandra et al. 2010
;
Poncé et al. 2015
;
Caruso et al. 2017
;
Verdi et al. 2017
;
Miglio et al. 2020
;
Girotto and Novko 2023
.
Second, calculations are in practice limited to a sparse sampling of the
BZ
(
BZ
) or, equivalently, short-range interatomic force constants.
This poses a problem for materials close to a lattice instability, signaled by Kohn anomalies
Kohn 1959
driven by a strong long-range electronic response.
Furthermore, the exchange-correlation energy is described by an approximate functional.
Thus,
DFT
-based methods will inevitably fail for strongly correlated materials where the single-electron picture breaks down
Hubbard and Flowers 1963
.

A popular approach in cases where the abovementioned approximations cannot be applied is to amend the results through suitable corrections to the self-energy of the electrons
Geĭlikman 1971
;
Koçer et al. 2020
and phonons
Brovman and Kagan 1967
;
Ipatova and Subashiev 1974
;
Falter and Selmke 1981
;
Falter 1988
;
Calandra et al. 2010
;
Giustino 2017
.
Here, one usually faces a difficulty referred to as “double counting” or “overscreening”
Paleari and Marini 2021a
;
Paleari and Marini 2021b
;
Marini 2023a
:
An effect that is accounted for in
DFT
must be removed before a more elaborate description of the same effect can be applied.
This is closely related to the concept of “downfolding”
Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
van Loon et al. 2021a
;
van Loon et al. 2021b
:
The full problem is mapped to an
ab initio
low-energy effective system with a significantly reduced number of degrees of freedom
Imada and Miyake 2010
.
Established downfolding methods for the electron-electron and electron-phonon interactions
Giovannetti et al. 2014
are the
cRPA
(
cRPA
)
Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Aryasetiawan et al. 2006
and
cDFPT
(
cDFPT
)
Nomura and Arita 2015
, respectively.
While for the former several implementations in popular simulation software exist
Friedrich et al. 2010
;
Şaşıoğlu et al. 2013
;
Amadon et al. 2014
;
Kaltak 2015
;
Nakamura et al. 2021
, a general workflow for the latter is at an earlier development stage
Nomura and Arita 2015
;
Berges et al. 2020
;
Novko 2020
.

In this work, we revisit the problem of obtaining adiabatic and nonadiabatic phonons at a low electronic temperature that are converged with respect to the sampling of the
BZ
, originally addressed in Ref.
2
.
In doing so, we also react to the recently revived controversy about the correct screening of the electron-phonon vertices in the phonon self-energy:
It is universally acknowledged that both perturbative
Reichardt 2018
;
Paleari and Marini 2021a
;
Paleari and Marini 2021b
;
Marini 2023a
and nonperturbative treatments of the problem based on the Hedin-Baym equations
Giustino 2017
yield a phonon self-energy with one bare and one screened vertex.
Still, there are strong arguments
Calandra et al. 2010
that the traditional choice
Allen 1972
;
Lazzeri and Mauri 2006
;
Pisana et al. 2007
;
Giustino et al. 2007a
;
Novko 2018
;
Novko et al. 2020
of two screened vertices can not only be ascribed to the fact that these are readily available from
DFPT
, but is indeed preferable for phonon self-energy
differences
as long as the screened vertex is approximated at the
DFT
level.

Here, by comparing the two approaches and supporting them with numerical results for monolayer TaS
2
and n-doped MoS
2
, we will detail the following.
(i) Working with one bare vertex is exact and thus allows for systematic improvements.
However, the quality of the result depends on the achievable precision of the screened vertex.
The computational cost is increased by the necessity to sum over many electronic bands, but a possible way around this problem has recently been published
Lihm and Park 2021
.
Also, the bare vertex is not routinely obtained in a pseudopotential framework.
In practice, some authors have approximated the bare vertex by
unscreening
the
DFPT
vertex with model dielectric functions
Caruso et al. 2017
;
Prasad Kafle et al. 2020
, but their accuracy is limited by the validity of these models.
(ii) The established approach with two screened vertices
Calandra et al. 2010
yields excellent results at low cost for a wide parameter range.
Its robustness can be explained by a designed cancellation of errors to first order.

We also illustrate a third option (iii) based on downfolding via
cDFPT

Nomura and Arita 2015
, which can be shown to be equivalent to (i) with a computational cost similar to (ii).
By splitting the electronic transitions into an “active subspace” and its complement, the “rest subspace
,
” we can gradually switch between the bare and the screened vertex and settle for the optimum.
Whenever we deal with strongly correlated materials
Savrasov and Kotliar 2003
;
Koçer et al. 2020
or wish to also describe the frequency dependence of the electron-phonon vertex
Giustino 2017
;
Reichardt 2018
, which is beyond the scope of this work, it becomes necessary to explicitly consider the electron-electron interaction.
We believe that in these cases downfolding via a combination of
cRPA
and
cDFPT
, as opposed to (i), is a viable option because it provides a complexity reduction without introducing a double-counting problem.
In fact, within such a framework, the ideas of (ii) can still be applied
1
1

1

Francesco Mauri (private communication).
.

Importantly, not only the bare but also the partially screened quantities are, in good approximation, adiabatic—in an Engelsberg-Schrieffer sense
Engelsberg and Schrieffer 1963
;
Saitta et al. 2008
—and independent of the electronic temperature since the rest system is gapped.
As a consequence, they are also smooth in large parts of reciprocal space, which facilitates interpolation and ensures convergence already at coarse
BZ
sampling.
Nevertheless, there is also a drawback since the absence of these screening effects is accompanied by the emergence of long-range Fröhlich terms.
Since the associated discontinuities in the derivatives of phonon dispersion and electron-phonon coupling render a straightforward Fourier interpolation impossible, an accurate description of these long-range terms, which allows us to subtract and add them before and after interpolation, is needed.

We implemented approaches (i), (ii), and (iii) in
Quantum ESPRESSO

Giannozzi et al. 2009
;
Giannozzi et al. 2017
;
Giannozzi et al. 2020
and the
EPW
code
Giustino et al. 2007b
;
Noffsinger et al. 2010
;
Poncé et al. 2016
, see Supplemental Material
2
2

2

See Supplemental Material at
https://arxiv.org/src/2212.11806/anc
for the source code and data associated with this work.
It is also available in the Materials Cloud Archive at
https://doi.org/10.24435/materialscloud:he-pv
.
,
independently of an existing realization of (ii)
Marini et al. 2023
.
On this basis, we can efficiently study the fine features of Kohn anomalies
He et al. 2020
, which arise from the active subspace, at the required (and otherwise prohibitive) ultradense
BZ
sampling and with frequency dependence.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Sec.
II
, we present the relevant theoretical concepts and formulas, which are illustrated using simple models in Sec.
III
.
Subsequently, in Sec.
IV
, we describe our
ab initio
implementation, results from which are shown in Sec.
V
.
We finish by summarizing our work and discussing possible generalization of the scheme in Sec.
VI
.

II
Theory

In this section, we review the theoretical background, including the phonon Green’s function (Sec.
II.1
), downfolding (Sec.
II.2
), the diagrams describing the screening of phonons and interactions (Sec.
II.3
), approximate phonon self-energies (Sec.
II.4
), their symmetrization (Sec.
II.5
), Friedel and Fröhlich long-range effects (Sec.
II.6
), and the basics of Wannier-Fourier interpolation (Sec.
II.7
).
We employ Hartree atomic units where
m_{\textnormal{e}}=e=\hbar=4\pi\epsilon_{0}=1
.

II.1
Phonon Green’s function and dynamical matrix

The lattice dynamics of a material can be described by the phonon Green’s function or, more precisely, the retarded displacement-displacement correlation function
3
3

3

Strictly speaking, the phonon Green’s function is the correlation function of phonon ladder operators instead of displacement operators
Giustino 2017
and thus differs from Eq. (
1
) by a factor of
2\omega
, which thus appears in Eq. (
3
) for the phonon spectral function.
:

G_{\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}(T,t-t^{\prime})=-\mathrm{i}\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\sqrt{M_{\kappa}M_{\kappa^{\prime}}}\\
\times\langle[\hat{u}_{\mathbf{R}\kappa\alpha}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}(t),\hat{u}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})]\rangle_{T},

(1)

where the Heaviside function
\Theta
ensures that a displacement introduced at time
t^{\prime}
can only propagate forward in time
t>t^{\prime}
,
u
and
M
are atomic displacements and masses,
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
are Bravais lattice vectors,
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
and
\alpha,\beta
enumerate basis atoms and Cartesian directions,
\langle\cdots\rangle_{T}
denotes an ensemble average at electronic temperature
T
, and
[{\cdots},{\cdots}]
is the commutator.
Knowledge of the correlations between any two atomic displacements at different times and positions as in Eq. (
1
) completely characterizes the lattice dynamics.

Because of translational invariance, Eq. (
1
) depends on
differences
of times
t-t^{\prime}
and lattice vectors
\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}\mathrlap{{}^{\prime}}
, which allows for a Fourier transform to frequency
\omega
and phonon momentum
\mathbf{q}
,

G_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=\frac{\mathds{1}}{\omega^{2}\mathds{1}-D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)},

(2)

with the screened dynamical matrix
D
.
Here,
\omega
is defined on the whole complex plane and the
retarded
phonon Green’s function is obtained at
\omega+\mathrm{i}0^{+}
with
\omega
real and
0^{+}
a positive infinitesimal.
The right-hand side of Eq. (
2
) is a matrix inversion in the basis of displacements
\kappa\alpha,\kappa^{\prime}\beta
, and
\mathds{1}
is the corresponding identity matrix.

The link to experiments such as inelastic neutron- or x-ray-scattering spectroscopy
Caruso et al. 2017
is the phonon spectral function.
It assigns an intensity to each combination of energies
\omega
and crystal momenta
\mathbf{q}
and thus provides the quasiparticle band structure, where applicable, including many-body effects such as broadening and satellites.
We compute the phonon spectral function as
Abrikosov et al. 1963

A_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=-\frac{2\omega}{\pi}\Tr\Im G_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega+\mathrm{i}0^{+}).

(3)

Instead, the static (
\omega=0
) adiabatic phonon frequencies
\omega_{\mathbf{q}\nu}
as obtained from
DFPT
, usually at high electronic smearing
\sigma
, follow from the eigenvalue equation for the dynamical matrix:

D_{\mathbf{q}}(\sigma,0)\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{q}\nu}^{\vphantom{0}}=\omega^{2}_{\mathbf{q}\nu}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{q}\nu}^{\vphantom{0}},

(4)

where
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{q}\nu}
are the phonon eigenvectors with mode index
\nu
.

II.2
Screened, partially screened, and bare quantities

In a typical
ab initio
calculation, all electronic states are treated equally.
However, the relevant physics often takes place in a small subset of these states, namely the low-energy states close to the Fermi level, which we refer to as active states.
In particular, if an
ab initio
calculation for chosen parameters and approximations fails to converge or capture the effects of interest, this is likely due to processes within this subset of active states alone, while the rest might already be properly described.
Before resorting to more elaborate treatments, it is thus instructive to consider the different subsets of states and corresponding energy scales separately
Falter and Selmke 1981
;
Falter 1988
.

While pure
ab initio
approaches always address the full system, the downfolding approach
Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Aryasetiawan et al. 2006
;
Giovannetti et al. 2014
;
Nomura and Arita 2015
uses
ab initio
calculations to construct tractable low-energy systems with active states only, thus reducing the number of degrees of freedom significantly.
This approach is exact, provided the complexity reduction is properly compensated by the partial screening of the system parameters.
While the full system consists of simple
bare
elementary particles and interactions, the parameters of the downfolded system usually acquire dependences on the involved quantum numbers and are in general also frequency dependent
Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Falter et al. 2006
.
The chosen active states must thus span an energy window large enough that low-energy effects and related dependences can be safely neglected and at the same time small enough to keep the computational cost affordable.

Figure 1:
Visualization of active (A) and rest (R) subspaces of electronic transitions based on a generic band structure.
A only includes transitions between a chosen set of low-energy states highlighted in orange, R all remaining transitions.
Note that there is in general an infinite number of empty states beyond the shown energy range.

A typical choice of active and rest states is sketched in Fig.
1
.
Note that here, for convenience but departing from what is usually done, we define the active subspace not as the set of active
states
but as the set of
transitions
between them.

The full system with bare parameters would be recovered if
all
states were counted among the active states.
Hence, special care has to be taken in the context of pseudopotentials.
Since quasiparticle energies and interactions decrease with screening, the bare quantities in an all-electron calculation are larger than those in a pseudopotential framework with less core states.

II.3
Random-phase approximation (RPA)

In this section, we review the formulas that describe the electronic screening of phonons and interactions in the framework of the
RPA
(
RPA
)
Bohm and Pines 1951
;
Gell-Mann and Brueckner 1957
;
Ren et al. 2012
;
van Loon et al. 2021b
.
In this approximation, the electronic response is determined by the bare polarizability of the system.
In terms of Feynman diagrams, we consider all possible diagrams consisting of bare phonons, the bare electron-phonon interaction, the bare electron-electron interaction, and electron-hole “bubbles
.
”
In the static case (
\omega=0
), this is equivalent to the screening in
DFPT
as long as the electrons are given by the adiabatically screened Kohn-Sham states and the exchange-correlation kernel is included in the bare electron-electron interaction
Nomura and Arita 2015
;
Giustino 2017
.
A summary of the most relevant symbols used in the following is provided in Table
1
.

II.3.1
Electron-electron interaction

We start with the screening of the electron-electron interaction, for which the
RPA
has been originally derived
Bohm and Pines 1951
.
In the basis of electronic eigenstates, used throughout the paper, the bare Coulomb interaction can be written as

v_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}=\langle\mathbf{k}{+}\mathbf{q}m;\mathbf{k}^{\prime}n^{\prime}|\hat{v}|\mathbf{k}n;\mathbf{k}^{\prime}{+}\mathbf{q}m^{\prime}\rangle,

(5)

with electron momentum
\mathbf{k}
, band indices
m,n
, and the virtual photon momentum transfer
\mathbf{q}
.
We purposely used the symbol
\mathbf{q}
as in Eq. (
2
) to highlight the fact that in
RPA
the phonon and photon momentum transfers have to be the same—this is no longer true beyond
RPA
.
More precisely, Eq. (
5
) quantifies the scattering of two electrons from single-particle states
|\mathbf{k}n\rangle
and
|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}{+}\mathbf{q}m^{\prime}\rangle
into states
|\mathbf{k}{+}\mathbf{q}m\rangle
and
|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}n^{\prime}\rangle
, respectively.
In the basis of electronic positions, it has the well-known diagonal representation
\langle\mathbf{r};\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|\hat{v}|\mathbf{r};\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\rangle=1/|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|
.

The interaction between two electrons is, however, screened by the polarizability of all other electrons.
Taking the formation of any number of electron-hole pairs into account, the screened electron-electron interaction
W(T,\omega)
is related to the bare Coulomb interaction
v
via

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(6)

where
\mathbf{q}
is transferred from the right to the left.
As a formula,

W_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}(T,\omega)=v_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}+\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime\prime}n^{\prime\prime}}v_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime\prime}n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\times\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime\prime}n^{\prime\prime}}^{\textnormal{b}}(T,\omega)W_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}m^{\prime\prime}n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega),

(7)

where the summation is over
all
pairs of band indices.

W(T,\omega)
as defined here is also used in the
GW
approximation
Hedin 1965
;
Golze et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2019
.
The summation over an infinite number of bands can be circumvented via the Sternheimer approach
Sternheimer 1954
;
Giustino et al. 2010
;
Schlipf et al. 2020
;
Lihm and Park 2021
.

The bare electronic susceptibility or “polarizability
,
” from which any
T
or
\omega
dependence originates, reads

\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}(T,\omega)=\frac{2}{N}\frac{f(\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}n}/T)-f(\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}m}/T)}{\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}n}-\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}m}+\omega},

(8)

where
\varepsilon
and
f
are the electronic energies and occupations, the factor 2 accounts for the spin, and
N
is the number of
\mathbf{k}
points.

The most important contributions in Eq. (
8
) come from transitions between occupied and empty states with similar energies, i.e., from the low-energy electrons near the Fermi level.
This suggests to split the screening in Eq. (
6
) into two steps, namely the
downfolding
to a low-energy system and the
renormalization
to recover physical results.
We label the subset of transitions between properly chosen low-energy active states as A and the remaining transitions as the rest R (cf. Fig.
1
).
The bare susceptibility can then be decomposed as

\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}(T,\omega)=\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b,A}}(T,\omega)+\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b,R}}(T,\omega),

(9)

where the first and second term can only be nonzero if the transition from
|\mathbf{k}n\rangle
to
|\mathbf{k}{+}\mathbf{q}m\rangle
is part of A and R, respectively.

First, in the downfolding step, the partially screened electron-electron interaction
U(T,\omega)
is calculated from the bare Coulomb interaction
v
:

(10)

This is known as the
cRPA

Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Aryasetiawan et al. 2006
.
The corresponding formula is equivalent to Eq. (
7
), except that the summation is constrained to the transitions in R.
In general, the partially screened
U(T,\omega)
depends on
T
and
\omega

Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Falter et al. 2006
.
However, excluding low-energy transitions makes the system effectively gapped and the dependence on
T
and
\omega
can be controlled via the size of the active subspace
Miyake et al. 2009
.

Second, in the renormalization step, the screened
W(T,\omega)
can be recovered from the partially screened
U(T,\omega)

Aryasetiawan et al. 2004
;
Falter et al. 2006
,

(11)

The corresponding formula is again equivalent to Eq. (
7
), but now the summation is constrained to the finite number of active bands.
This allows us to evaluate the low-energy response, which requires a dense
BZ
sampling at low electronic temperature, at an affordable computational cost.

II.3.2
Electron-phonon interaction

The change of the electronic energies upon ionic displacements is also screened by the surrounding electrons.
Without this screening, the bare electron-phonon coupling in the electronic eigenbasis reads

g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{M_{\kappa}}}\langle\mathbf{k}{+}\mathbf{q}m|\frac{\partial\hat{V}^{\textnormal{b}}}{\partial u_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha}}|\mathbf{k}n\rangle,

(12)

with the bare external potential
V^{\textnormal{b}}
acting on an electron amid the ensemble of ions.
In the position representation, we have
\langle\mathbf{r}|\hat{V}^{\textnormal{b}}|\mathbf{r}\rangle=-\sum_{\mathbf{R}\kappa}Z_{\kappa}/|\mathbf{R}+\bm{\uptau}_{\kappa}+\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{R}\kappa}-\mathbf{r}|
, where
Z_{\kappa}
and
\bm{\uptau}
are ionic charges and equilibrium positions within the unit cell.

The screened electron-phonon interaction can be written as
g=\epsilon^{-1}g^{\textnormal{b}}
with the dielectric function
\epsilon=1-v\chi^{\textnormal{b}}
, similarly to the screened electron-electron interaction
W=\epsilon^{-1}v
of Eq. (
6
), or, using diagrams, as (cf. Appendix I of Ref.
85
)

(13)

Translated to a formula, it reads

g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega)=g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}+\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}v_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}^{\vphantom{0}}\\
\times\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}^{\textnormal{b}}(T,\omega)g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega).

(14)

Also the renormalization of the electron-phonon coupling can be split into two steps:
The partially screened
g^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)
is obtained from the bare
g^{\textnormal{b}}
,

(19)

In practice, these explicit definitions can be more convenient if
U(T,\omega)
and
W(T,\omega)
are known.

II.3.3
Phonons

Finally, we consider how electronic screening affects the phonons.
Without any electronic response, the bare interatomic force constants read
Baroni et al. 2001

C_{\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\textnormal{b}}=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}r\frac{\partial^{2}V^{\textnormal{b}}(\mathbf{r})}{\partial u_{\mathbf{R}\kappa\alpha}\partial u_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}\beta}}n(\mathbf{r})+\frac{\partial^{2}\varPhi}{\partial u_{\mathbf{R}\kappa\alpha}\partial u_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}\beta}},

(20)

with the electron density
n
and the classical electrostatic energy
\varPhi=1/2\sum_{\mathbf{R}\kappa\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}}Z_{\kappa}Z_{\kappa^{\prime}}/|\mathbf{R}+\bm{\uptau}_{\kappa}+\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{R}\kappa}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\bm{\uptau}_{\kappa^{\prime}}-\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}}|
of the ensemble of ions, which is often called Ewald energy.
The corresponding bare phonon Green’s function
G^{\textnormal{b}}
follows from the bare dynamical matrix
D^{\textnormal{b}}=C^{\textnormal{b}}/\sqrt{M_{\kappa}M_{\kappa^{\prime}}}
and Eq. (
2
).

As derived step by step in Sec. 5.1 of Ref.
86
, the screened phonon Green’s function
G(T,\omega)
satisfies

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(21)

or, using matrices in the basis of ionic displacements,

G_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=G_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}+G_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}\cdot\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega)\cdot G_{\mathbf{q}}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega),

(22)

where we have defined the phonon self-energy as an electron-hole bubble connected to a bare—to avoid double counting the Coulomb interaction—and a screened electron-phonon vertex:

\varPi_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}(T,\omega)\\
=\sum_{\mathbf{k}mn}\bar{g}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b}}(T,\omega)g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega).

(23)

The bar denotes the complex conjugate.
If we multiply Eq. (
22
) with the matrix inverses
(G^{\textnormal{b}})^{-1}
and
G^{-1}(T,\omega)
from the left and right, respectively, and insert Eq. (
2
), we are left with a simple additive formula for the screened dynamical matrix:

D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\vphantom{0}}(T,\omega).

(24)

Eventually, also the screening of the phonons can be split into two steps.
First, the partially screened
G^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)
is derived from the bare
G^{\textnormal{b}}
:

(25)

Just like Eq. (
21
), Eq. (
25
) can be rewritten as an additive equation for the dynamical matrix:

D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)=D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{R}}(T,\omega).

(26)

In the second step, the screened
G(T,\omega)
is retrieved from the partially screened

(27)

Again, this translates into a simple addition of dynamical matrix and phonon self-energy:

D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega).

(28)

The phonon self-energy can thus be decomposed in the same way as the bare susceptibility in Eq. (
9
) [cf. Eq. (57) of Ref.
34
or Eq. (4.29) of Ref.
35
]:

\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)=\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{R}}(T,\omega).

(29)

In the following, we will focus on corrections to the first term, which reflects the relevant physics of the active subspace.

II.4
Approximations to the phonon self-energy

As outlined in the previous section, the exact phonon self-energy [Eq. (
23
)] is calculated using one bare and one screened electron-phonon vertex.
However, expressions with two screened vertices are often used in practice.
In Ref.
2
, the connection between these two formulations is established:
Using Eq. (
13
), and leaving
T
and
\omega
dependences understood for brevity, we can recast the phonon self-energy as

g\mathrlap{{}^{\textnormal{b}}}
g
=
g\mathrlap{{}^{\textnormal{b}}}
\,\Big[
g\mathrlap{{}^{\textnormal{b}}}
+
v
g
\Big]

(30)

=
\Big[
v
g
+
g\mathrlap{{}^{\textnormal{b}}}
\Big]\,
\,\Big[
g\mathrlap{{}^{\textnormal{b}}}
+
v
g
\Big]
-
v
g
g

(31)

=
g
g
-
v
g
g

,

(32)

i.e., as a phonon self-energy with two screened vertices less a double-counting term [cf. Eq. (4.23) of Ref.
87
and Eq. (4.5) of Ref.
88
].
All of the above expressions evaluate to the same result as long as all their constituents are exact.
If however—for reasons that will become evident soon—each occurrence of the electron response
g\chi^{\textnormal{b}}
or
\chi^{\textnormal{b}}g
in Eqs. (
30
) and (
31
) (
only
the mauve parts) is replaced by an approximation to it, their values will differ.
Here, by construction Eq. (
31
) will deviate least from the exact value.
This is because its
partial
functional derivative with respect to
\chi^{\textnormal{b}}g
vanishes:

(34)

Equation (
31
) is a stationary functional of the electron response
Calandra et al. 2010
.
Consequently, an approximate electron response yields errors only at second order.
Hence, it appears to be a reasonable approximation to replace the electron response in Eq. (
31
) by the static (
\omega=0
) and high-smearing (
T=\sigma
) response we obtain from an
ab initio
calculation using
DFPT
:

(35)

Since the approximate double-counting term does not depend on
T
or
\omega
, we only have to focus on the first term when correcting
ab initio
phonon self-energies.

Using Eq. (
16
) and the fact that the partially screened
U(T,\omega)
and
g^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)
exclude low-energy screening and are thus only weakly
T
and
\omega
dependent (in the phononic energy range), a corresponding expression can be derived for the active-subspace phonon self-energy
\varPi^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega)
:

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{42.39163pt}{8.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(41)

With Eq. (
37
), the approach of Ref.
2
to approximate converged low-temperature nonadiabatic phonons based on adiabatic high-smearing calculations can be formulated as

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)\approx D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{u}}(\sigma,0)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega),

(42)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{u}}(\sigma,0)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}(\sigma,0)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{00}}(\sigma,0),

(43)

where we have defined the “unscreened” dynamical matrix
D^{\textnormal{u}}(\sigma,0)
.
Note that in the original publication, a slightly different approach via phonons at the high electronic temperature
T_{\infty}
, chosen such that they can be safely interpolated, is proposed.
Equation (
42
) is equivalent for
\sigma\approx T_{\infty}
.

With Eq. (
38
), the corresponding
cDFPT
-based formula is

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)\approx D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p0}}(T,\omega)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p0}}(T,\omega),

(44)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}(\sigma,0)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p0}}(\sigma,0),

(45)

where the partially screened dynamical matrix
D^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
is calculated via unscreening
D(\sigma,0)
from
DFPT
but could also be directly obtained from a self-consistent
cDFPT
calculation.
We note that the choice between unscreening and constraining is also relevant in other contexts
Nomura et al. 2012
.

A shortcoming of Eq. (
38
) is that the bare susceptibility is generally calculated at a different electronic temperature than the screened vertex.
Taking the temperature dependence of the screened vertex into account yields Eq. (
39
) and

D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)\approx D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p$T$}}(T,\omega)\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{p$T$}}(T,\omega).

(46)

This will be insightful but is useless in practice since
g(T,0)
is as computationally expensive as
D(T,0)
.

In the limit of an infinitely large active subspace, where the partially screened vertex becomes bare, and for a single electronic temperature
T=\sigma
, Eqs. (
44
) and (
45
) are equivalent to Eq. (145) of Ref.
1
.
In practice, the phonon self-energy with one bare vertex might also be calculated for a finite number of active bands as in Eq. (
40
), which leads to

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)\approx D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b0}}(T,\omega)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{ub}}(\sigma,0)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b0}}(T,\omega),

(47)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{ub}}(\sigma,0)

\displaystyle\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}(\sigma,0)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b0}}(\sigma,0).

(48)

Note that the unscreened
D^{\textnormal{ub}}\neq D^{\textnormal{b}}
for finite active subspaces.
Also here, a variant with temperature-corrected screened vertex can be studied.
With Eq. (
41
),

D_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)\approx D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b$T$}}(T,\omega)\equiv D_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{ub}}(\sigma,0)+\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b$T$}}(T,\omega).

(49)

Importantly, the quality of Eqs. (
42
)–(
49
) is not determined by how close the approximate phonon self-energies defined in Eqs. (
37
)–(
41
) are to the exact phonon self-energy—in fact, the absolute values can be quite different.
Instead, what matters is the difference
\varPi^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega)-\varPi^{\textnormal{A}}(\sigma,0)
, the deviations from which we can quantify.

For the asymmetric formulations using one bare or partially screened vertex we can readily recognize that

\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b0/p0}}(T,\omega)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b0/p0}}(\sigma,0)\approx\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(\sigma,0)\\
+\smash[b]{\sum_{\mathbf{k}mn}}\bar{g}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b/p}}(\sigma,0)\chi_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b,A}}(T,\omega)\\
\times[g(\sigma,0)-g(T,\omega)]_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn},

(50)

where the error in the phonon self-energy correction is
linear
both in the bare or partially screened vertex and in the error of the screened vertex.
Equation (
50
) becomes exact for
g^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)=g^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
, i.e., for a large enough active subspace.
Since the strength of the partially screened vertex increases with the size of the active subspace (asymptotically approaching the bare vertex), we expect larger errors in Eq. (
47
) than in Eq. (
44
), especially for small low-energy active subspaces.

For the symmetric formulation using two screened vertices, via the straightforward derivation in Appendix
A
, we can analogously show that

\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{00}}(\sigma,0)\approx\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(T,\omega)-\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{A}}(\sigma,0)\\
-1/N^{2}\smash[b]{\sum_{\mathclap{\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}}}[\overline{g(\sigma,0)-g(T,-\bar{\omega})}]_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}W_{\mathbf{q}\mathbf{k}mn\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}^{-1}(T,\omega)\\
\times[g(\sigma,0)-g(T,\omega)]_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}},

(51)

where the error is only
quadratic
in the error of the screened vertex and where
W^{-1}=v^{-1}\epsilon
is the inverse screened electron-electron interaction [cf. Eq. (
6
)].
Equation (
51
) becomes exact for
g^{\textnormal{p}}(T,\omega)=g^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
and
U(T,\omega)=U(\sigma,0)
[cf. Eq. (
36
)].

Finally, we remark that throughout the paper by the word “static” we mean
\omega=0
and by “dynamical” that a quantity has a frequency dependence
\omega
.
With the word “nonadiabatic” we refer to an Engelsberg-Schrieffer
Engelsberg and Schrieffer 1963
type of nonadiabaticity, i.e., a nonadiabatic electronic renormalization of
adiabatic
phonons
Saitta et al. 2008
;
Calandra et al. 2010
.
However, we still neglect adiabatic effects beyond the Migdal theorem
Migdal 1958
arising from vertex corrections, which are of the order
\sqrt{m_{\textnormal{e}}\smash{/}M}
and thus usually much smaller.

II.5
Symmetrization of the phonon self-energy

The approximate phonon self-energies
\varPi^{\textnormal{p0}}
and
\varPi^{\textnormal{b0}}
have been defined such that the screened vertex is situated on the right of the electron-hole bubble.
An equivalent definition with the screened vertex on the left is possible.
While for the exact
\varPi
both choices yield the same result, this is no longer true when the bubbles contained in
g
through Eq. (
13
) or Eq. (
16
) are approximated (cf. Fig. 3 of Ref.
38
).
To ensure that the static
\varPi^{\textnormal{b0/p0}}(T,0)
are Hermitian
Berges et al. 2020
and the dynamical
\varPi^{\textnormal{b0/p0}}(T,\omega)
fulfill the fluctuation-dissipation theorem
Marini 2023a
, for the rest of the paper we replace
\smash{\bar{g}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b/p}}}(\sigma,0)g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}(\sigma,0)
by

\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\bigl[\bar{g}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b/p}}(\sigma,0)g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}(\sigma,0)\\
+\bar{g}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{k}mn}^{\vphantom{0}}(\sigma,0)g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa^{\prime}\beta\mathbf{k}mn}^{\textnormal{b/p}}(\sigma,0)\bigr]

(52)

in Eqs. (
38
) and (
40
) and similarly in Eqs. (
39
) and (
41
).
Note that complex conjugation of the coupling inverts the scattering process in the static case where the bare susceptibility is real and invariant under
\mathbf{q}\leftrightarrow-\mathbf{q}
.
In the dynamical case, care has to be taken to keep the frequency argument in the correct quadrant of the complex plane [cf. Eq. (
51
)].

By construction,
\mathcal{G}
is a Hermitian matrix in the basis of displacements
\kappa\alpha,\kappa^{\prime}\beta
for each
\mathbf{q},\mathbf{k}
and
m,n
.
It has at most two nonzero eigenvalues
\lambda^{(1)}>\lambda^{(2)}
of opposite sign belonging to the normalized eigenvectors
\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}
, i.e., a spectral representation

\mathcal{G}_{\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}=\bar{g}^{(1)}_{\kappa\alpha\vphantom{\beta}}g^{(1)}_{\kappa^{\prime}\beta}-\bar{g}^{(2)}_{\kappa\alpha\vphantom{\beta}}g^{(2)}_{\kappa^{\prime}\beta},

(53)

with
\mathbf{g}^{(1,2)}=\sqrt{|\lambda^{(1,2)}|}\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}
.
As long as
g^{\textnormal{b/p}}
and
g
mostly differ in magnitude but not in direction, the negative eigenvalue is negligibly small and the phonon self-energy can be rewritten with the same effective vertex
g^{(1)}
on both sides.
This applies if the dielectric function is largely independent of or diagonal in the electronic indices.
For small
\mathbf{q}
, long-range effects that will be discussed in Sec.
II.6.2
enhance the orthogonal component of
g^{\textnormal{b/p}}
and thus the negative eigenvalue.

II.6
Long-range effects

II.6.1
Friedel long-rangedness

In a metal, the bare electronic susceptibility usually has sharp features at finite momenta in reciprocal space or, equivalently, it is long-range in real space.
This can be referred to as Friedel
Friedel 1958
, Peierls
Peierls 1955
, or Kohn
Kohn 1959
long-rangedness.
These features are generated by the low-energy system; i.e., they affect
\chi^{\textnormal{b,A}}(T,\omega)
while
\chi^{\textnormal{b,R}}(T,\omega)
[cf. Eq. (
9
)] can safely be assumed to be smooth in reciprocal space or short-range in real space.
This long-rangedness is inherited by the other screened quantities discussed in Sec.
II.3
, i.e.,
G(T,\omega)
or
D(T,\omega)
,
g(T,\omega)
, and
W(T,\omega)
.
All remaining quantities are bare or partially screened and thus smooth, which allows us not only to calculate them using coarse integration meshes but also to interpolate them easily.
We show an example of Friedel long-range effects for a one-dimensional model in Fig.
2
(a).

II.6.2
Fröhlich long-rangedness

The bare and partially screened phonons and electron-phonon coupling do not show any Friedel long-rangedness.
However, as soon as the metallic screening is removed, we deal with another type of long-range phenomenon usually observed in insulators and semiconductors, which we will refer to as Fröhlich or Coulomb long-rangedness, most clearly recognizable in the bare Coulomb interaction
v(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=1/|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|
itself.
In the absence of metallic screening, this propagates into the bare or partially screened quantities and manifests as divergences or discontinuities at the center of the
BZ
, which would lead to Gibbs oscillations when performing a naive Fourier interpolation.
However, analytical models for these effects exist
Brovman and Kagan 1967
;
Giannozzi et al. 1991
;
Gonze et al. 1994
;
Verdi and Giustino 2015
;
Sjakste et al. 2015
;
Sohier et al. 2016
;
Royo et al. 2020
;
Brunin et al. 2020
;
Jhalani et al. 2020
;
Poncé et al. 2021
;
Royo and Stengel 2021
;
Macheda et al. 2022
;
Macheda et al. 2023
;
Sio and Giustino 2022
, allowing us to split the electron-phonon coupling and the dynamical matrix into a long-range (
\mathcal{L}
) and a short-range part (
\mathcal{S}
), the latter of which can be easily interpolated.

Here, we will apply a recent approach to model the long-range electrostatics of two-dimensional materials
Royo and Stengel 2021
;
Poncé et al. 2023a
;
Poncé et al. 2023b
, including dipolar and quadrupolar terms, where we neglect the effect of the out-of-plane polarizability on the dynamical matrix.
We decompose the bare or partially screened dynamical matrix and electron-phonon coupling as

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\textnormal{b/p}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{S}}+D_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{L}},

(54)

\displaystyle g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{R}sp}^{\textnormal{b/p}}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{R}sp}^{\mathcal{S}}+g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha}^{\mathcal{L}}\delta_{\smash{\mathbf{R}0}}^{\vphantom{\mathcal{L}}}\delta_{sp}^{\vphantom{\mathcal{L}}},

(55)

where
s
and
p
label electronic orbitals located in the unit cells at the origin and
\mathbf{R}
, respectively.
The long-range parts read

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{D}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}\sum_{\kappa^{\prime\prime}}\widetilde{D}_{0\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{L}},

(56)

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\kappa^{\prime}\beta}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathclap{\mathbf{G}\neq-\mathbf{q}}}a_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}}\bar{b}_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}\kappa\alpha}b_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}\kappa^{\prime}\beta},

(57)

\displaystyle g_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathclap{\mathbf{G}\neq-\mathbf{q}}}a_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}}b_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}\kappa\alpha},

(58)

where the summations over
in plane
reciprocal lattice vectors
\mathbf{G}
converge fast.
The scalar part (independent of
\kappa
and
\alpha
) is

a_{\mathbf{q}}=\frac{2\pi f_{L}(\mathbf{q})}{A|\mathbf{q}|}\bigl[1+\frac{cf_{L}(\mathbf{q})}{2|\mathbf{q}|}\mathbf{q}^{T}(\epsilon-\mathds{1})\mathbf{q}\bigr]^{-1},

(59)

with the unit cell area
A
and height
c
, the dielectric constant
\epsilon
, and the cutoff function
f_{L}(\mathbf{q})=1-\tanh(|\mathbf{q}|L/2)
.
The range-separation parameter
L
is chosen such that the real-space force constants are minimized.
The vectorial part is given by

b_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{q}\bm{\uptau}_{\kappa}}}{\sqrt{M_{\kappa}}}\bigl[\mathrm{i}\mathbf{Z}^{*}_{\kappa\alpha}\mathbf{q}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{q}^{T}Q_{\kappa\alpha}\mathbf{q}\bigr],

(60)

including the Born effective charges
Z^{*}_{\kappa\alpha\beta}
and the quadrupole tensors
Q_{\kappa\alpha\beta\beta^{\prime}}
.
Note that neither phonons nor effective charges from
cDFPT
calculations fulfill the acoustic sum rule.
In the bare system,
\epsilon=\mathds{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{*}=Z\mathds{1}
with nuclear charge
Z
.

II.7
Wannierization and Fourier interpolation

In practice, calculations of the electronic energies and especially the dynamical matrices and electron-phonon couplings are limited to a coarse grid of
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{q}
points in the
BZ
.
The points in between are usually obtained via Fourier interpolation, i.e., by a discrete Fourier transform into a localized representation and the smoothest possible back transform to arbitrary points.
The phononic degrees of freedom already have a natural localized basis, namely the Cartesian ionic displacement directions; for the electrons, the basis of Wannier functions
Marzari et al. 2012
, i.e., localized orthogonal orbitals, is used.

For instance, the interpolant of the short-range part of the electron-phonon coupling
Giustino et al. 2007b
as used in the
EPW
code
Giustino et al. 2007b
;
Noffsinger et al. 2010
;
Poncé et al. 2016
reads

g_{\mathbf{q}\nu\mathbf{k}mn}^{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\mathclap{\mathbf{R}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{R}^{\prime}sp}}e_{\mathbf{q}\kappa\alpha\nu}^{\vphantom{\mathcal{S}}}\bar{\psi}_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}sm}^{\vphantom{\mathcal{S}}}g_{\mathbf{R}\kappa\alpha\mathbf{R}^{\prime}sp}^{\mathcal{S}}\psi_{\mathbf{k}pn}^{\vphantom{\mathcal{S}}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{q}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{k}\mathbf{R}^{\prime})},

(61)

where
e
and
\psi
are the eigenvectors of dynamical matrix and Wannier Hamiltonian, for which equivalent formulas hold.

Having handled the Fröhlich long-rangedness (Sec.
II.6.2
), we can safely interpolate all quantities except those related to
\chi^{\textnormal{b,A}}(T,\omega)
at low
T
, which has to be evaluated on a dense mesh because of the inherent Friedel long-rangedness (Sec.
II.6.1
).

III
Model results

Inspired by Ref.
38
, to illustrate selected formulas given in the previous section, we will now apply them to minimal models for which the dynamical phonon self-energy
\varPi(T,\omega)
in
RPA
as defined in Eq. (
23
) is accessible.
First, in Sec.
III.1
, we consider a one-dimensional chain with nearest-neighbor hopping parameter, force constant, and electron-phonon coupling, similar to what has been used by Peierls
Peierls 1955
and Su
et al.

Su et al. 1979
.
Second, in Sec.
III.2
, we consider the lowest band of a periodic homogeneous electron gas with Fröhlich electron-phonon coupling.
We complement both models with suitable electron-electron interactions to realize different screening levels of the electron-phonon coupling.
As there is only one electronic band, which is considered as active, no distinction between bare and partially screened quantities is made here.

III.1
Generalized one-dimensional Peierls model

Figure 2:
Results for the generalized (a–c) one-dimensional Peierls and (d) three-dimensional Fröhlich models.
(a) Adiabatically screened phonon dispersion in
RPA
with Kohn anomaly for different electronic temperatures
T
at half filling.
The inset depicts the corresponding interatomic force constants, where dashed lines are guides for the eye.
(b) Phonon spectral function in
RPA
together with adiabatic phonon dispersion at
T=25
meV at half filling (orange) and one-third filling (mauve).
(c) Cross section through the spectral function at the Kohn anomaly according to Eq. (
24
) (
RPA
), Eq. (
42
) (“screened-screened”), and Eq. (
47
) (“bare-screened”).
(d) Corresponding figure for the Fröhlich model for a selected Fermi wave vector
k_{\textnormal{F}}
and phonon momentum
q
.
The inset depicts the linewidth according to Eq. (
70
) as a function of
q
.

We model the electron and phonon dispersion as well as the electron-phonon and electron-electron interaction as

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}

\displaystyle=-t\cos(k),

(62)

\displaystyle D_{q}^{\textnormal{b}}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}^{2}[1-\cos(q)],

(63)

\displaystyle g_{qk}(T,0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}g_{0}[\sin(k)-\sin(k+q)],

(64)

\displaystyle U_{q}

\displaystyle=U_{0}-V_{0}\log[\sin(|q|/2)],

(65)

where we choose
t=1
eV,
\omega_{0}=50
meV,
g_{0}=0.02
eV
3/2
, a local
U_{0}=1
eV, and a nonlocal
V_{0}=0.5
eV.
At half filling, the Fermi wave vector is
k_{\textnormal{F}}=\pi/2
and the chemical potential zero.

Note that while
D^{\textnormal{b}}
and
U
are bare, we define an adiabatically screened
g(T,0)
, from which the bare
g^{\textnormal{b}}
follows through unscreening using Eq. (
16
).
The screened
W(T,\omega)
,
g(T,\omega)
, and
D(T,\omega)
are then obtained via Eqs. (
6
), (
17
), and (
21
).
Our choice for the coupling has the disadvantage that
g^{\textnormal{b}}
rather than
g
depends on electronic temperature
T
and the filling
k_{\textnormal{F}}
via a
k
-independent “on site” term.
This however ensures a physically sound
g(T,0)
—describing the displacement-induced change of the hopping parameter
t
—and does not invalidate our central findings, displayed in Fig.
2
(a–c).

In Fig.
2
(a), we show the relation between static low-energy electronic screening and long-range force constants at half filling, as discussed in Sec.
II.6.1
.
The force constants are obtained from
D(T,0)
via a discrete Fourier transform.
At the high electronic temperature of
T=1
eV, the phonon dispersion is smooth and the corresponding force constants decay to nearly zero already after the first neighboring site.
As soon as the electronic temperature is lowered, a Kohn anomaly
Kohn 1959
in the phonon dispersion emerges at the
BZ
boundary
q=2k_{\textnormal{F}}=\pi
, and the force constants become long-range exhibiting Friedel oscillations
Friedel 1958
, whereby the sign changes from one site to the other.
For the chosen parameters, the phonons become soft (imaginary frequencies) below the
CDW
(
CDW
) temperature
T_{\textnormal{CDW}}=6.16
meV, indicating the onset of the dimerization.

We now turn to the nonadiabatic case.
Figure
2
(b) shows the phonon spectral function in
RPA
using Eq. (
3
) at
T=25
meV for
k_{\textnormal{F}}=\pi/2
(orange shade) and
k_{\textnormal{F}}=\pi/3
(mauve shade), together with the corresponding adiabatic phonon frequencies (solid lines).
In practice, we replace the infinitesimal
0^{+}
by a small value of
0.4
meV and sample the
BZ
using 55 440
k
points.
There are no noteworthy nonadiabatic effects in the vicinity of the
\Gamma
point, toward which the coupling vanishes.
However, when approaching the Kohn anomaly, whose position
q=2k_{\textnormal{F}}
moves away from the
BZ
boundary upon doping, the branch starts to broaden significantly.

In Fig.
2
(c), we show cross sections through the phonon spectral function at the Kohn anomaly.
We compare the results using
\varPi(T,\omega)
in
RPA
[Eq. (
23
)] (solid lines) with corresponding data from
\varPi^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega)
[Eq. (
37
)] (dashed lines) and
\varPi^{\textnormal{b0}}(T,\omega)
[Eq. (
40
)] (dotted lines).
Here, we first calculate
D(T,0)
in static
RPA
, to which we add phonon self-energy
differences
according to Eqs. (
42
) and (
47
).
As we do not change the electronic temperature, no distinction between low
\sigma
and high
T
is made.
While at half filling all approaches agree since
g=g^{\textnormal{b}}
by symmetry, away from it they start to deviate.
The approach using two screened vertices remains close to the
RPA
result, while the combination of bare and approximately screened vertices overestimates the linewidth.
This is consistent with the linear and quadratic errors quantified in Sec.
II.4
.
Indeed, subtracting the second and third lines of Eqs. (
50
) and (
51
), accessible for this model, from the approximate
D^{\textnormal{b0}}(T,\omega)
and
D^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega)
, respectively, we recover the
RPA
result.
The quadratic error simplifies to
\langle g_{qk}(T,0)-g_{qk}(T,\omega)\rangle_{k}^{2}/W_{q}(T,\omega)
, where
\langle\cdots\rangle_{k}
denotes the
k
average.

III.2
Generalized three-dimensional Fröhlich model

As a second model, similar to the one considered in Sec. V C of Ref.
38
, we now consider the lowest band of a periodic homogeneous electron gas,

\varepsilon_{\mathbf{k}}=\frac{1}{2m^{*}}\min_{\mathbf{G}}|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}|^{2},

(66)

with an effective mass
m^{*}=2m_{\textnormal{e}}
in a cell of lattice constant
a=10
Å (defining the reciprocal lattice vectors
\mathbf{G}
), a constant Einstein phonon of frequency

\omega_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}=\omega_{0},

(67)

with
\omega_{0}=25
meV, the Fröhlich electron-phonon coupling

g_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{b}}=\mathrm{i}g_{0}\max_{\mathbf{G}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}|},

(68)

with
g_{0}=55
meV
3/2
/Å, and the Coulomb interaction

U_{\mathbf{q}}=U_{0}\max_{\mathbf{G}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{G}|^{2}},

(69)

with
U_{0}=5
meV/Å
2
.
We choose
k_{\textnormal{F}}=\pi/2a
.

Our model differs from the one of Ref.
38
in the choice of parameters, which in our case do not describe MgB
2
.
Also, Ref.
38
considers the full
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{q}
dependence of the extended system.
We obtain the screened quantities as described in the previous section, Sec.
III.1
, this time using
T=\eta=3
meV in combination with
240^{3}

\mathbf{k}
points.
In addition, we approximate the phonon linewidth as
Marini 2023a

\gamma_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{(00/b0)}}=-\frac{1}{\omega_{0}}\Im\varPi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\textnormal{(00/b0)}}(\omega_{0}+\mathrm{i}\eta).

(70)

The results are shown in Fig.
2
(d).
Interestingly, for the selected phonon momentum
q\equiv q_{x}=k_{\textnormal{F}}/2
, the phonon self-energy in
RPA
yields a nontrivial phonon line shape with a satellite at about 68 meV (solid line).
This feature is not captured by the screened-screened approach (dashed line), which means that it is encoded in the infinite series of dynamical electron-hole bubbles included in
g(T,\omega)
.
However, the main phonon peak at about 13 meV is relatively well reproduced.
Again, the bare-screened approach (dotted line) overestimates the linewidth.
This can also be seen in the inset, where
\gamma
from Eq. (
70
) is shown as a function of
q
.
As the coupling in this model does not depend on
\mathbf{k}
, the quadratic error of
D^{\textnormal{00}}(T,\omega)
simplifies even more and reads
[g_{\mathbf{q}}(T,0)-g_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)]^{2}/W_{\mathbf{q}}(T,\omega)
.

The findings presented above suggest that the approach of Ref.
2
can indeed be used to estimate the frequency-dependent dynamical matrix based on adiabatic results.
It is however not clear to what extent the conclusions drawn from these models can be transferred to realistic materials simulated
ab initio
.
This question will be addressed in the following sections.
Since here, different from the model case, nonadiabatic reference data are missing, we will not consider the transition from the static to the dynamical case but rather from high to low electronic temperature.

IV
Implementation

We implemented routines to perform
cDFPT
calculations, based on existing code provided by the authors of Ref.
3
, and to renormalize the phonons according to Eqs. (
42
)–(
49
) in the
PHonon
and
EPW
codes
Giustino et al. 2007b
;
Noffsinger et al. 2010
;
Poncé et al. 2016
, which are part of the
Quantum ESPRESSO
distribution
Giannozzi et al. 2009
;
Giannozzi et al. 2017
;
Giannozzi et al. 2020
.
The corresponding patch is provided in Supplemental Material
Note2
.

The implementation of constrained theories such as
cRPA
and
cDFPT
on top of existing programs to perform unconstrained
RPA
or
DFPT
calculations is straightforward and requires only minor modifications of the source code
Nomura and Arita 2015
.
In fact, the most difficult aspect is the definition of suitable electronic active subspaces, i.e., the identification of the band indices (usually sorted by energy in
ab initio
codes) that belong to the active bands for each
\mathbf{k}
point.
In fortunate cases, an appropriate low-energy subspace is isolated from all other bands
Berges et al. 2020
.
In the general case, however, the active bands will be entangled with other bands.

For simplicity, we define the
cDFPT
active subspace via an energy window, but a selection via band indices or orbital projections is also possible
Berges et al. 2017
.
We use a slightly modified version of the
PHonon
code with additional input parameters
cdfpt_min
and
cdfpt_max
, which define the lower and upper bounds of the energy window, as well as
bare
to suppress the electronic response.

The calculation and interpolation of the electron-phonon coupling as well as the phonon renormalization are done with the
EPW
code
Giustino et al. 2007b
;
Noffsinger et al. 2010
;
Poncé et al. 2016
.
Usually, the
EPW
code reads a single directory
dvscf_dir
including the dynamical matrices
D
and the change of the self-consistent potential
\partial V
from
DFPT
as calculated with the
PHonon
code.
We define a second input parameter
cdfpt_dir
pointing to analogous data
D^{\textnormal{b/p}}
and
\partial V^{\textnormal{b/p}}
from a bare or
cDFPT
calculation.
This directory also contains the values of
cdfpt_min
and
cdfpt_max
, which for convenience are used to set the default “frozen window” for the generation of Wannier functions
Pizzi et al. 2020
.
If both
dvscf_dir
and
cdfpt_dir
are specified, the modified code performs the calculation of the electron-matrix elements
g(\sigma,0)
and
g^{\textnormal{b/p}}(\sigma,0)
and the Fourier interpolation of the dynamical matrices
D(\sigma,0)
and
D^{\textnormal{b/p}}(\sigma,0)
and the matrix elements in the same way.
The fact that identical basis transforms are employed on both the
DFPT
and the
cDFPT
data ensures a consistent gauge.
Finally, we evaluate phonon self-energies and spectral functions for arbitrary
\mathbf{q}
points using dense
\mathbf{k}
meshes and small electronic temperatures.

Besides the above, we introduced additional inputs for the
EPW
code:
Since the
cDFPT
quantities do not always fulfill the acoustic sum rule
van Loon et al. 2021a
, enforcing it can be disabled using
asr_typ = 'none'
.
To properly handle the long-range terms in
cDFPT
, we define
lpolarc
and read the file
quadrupolec.fmt
in addition to the existing
lpolar
and
quadrupole.fmt
.
Using
unscreen_fine
,
D^{\textnormal{u}}(\sigma,0)
and
D^{\textnormal{ub/p}}(\sigma,0)
can be calculated on the dense instead of coarse
BZ
meshes [cf. Eqs. (
43
), (
45
), and (
48
)].
We select
T
and the corresponding smearing function
f
via
temps
and
types
.
Finally, the
\mathrm{i}0^{+}
appearing in the phonon spectral function in Eq. (
3
) is set in practice via two smearings,
degaussw
and
degaussq
, using Eq. (
79
) defined later.

V
Ab initio
results

In this section, we apply the above to monolayer TaS
2
, for which we calculate screened, partially screened, and bare phonons (Sec.
V.1
), the corresponding electron-phonon coupling (Sec.
V.2
), renormalized phonons using the different approaches (Secs.
V.3
and
V.4
), and the phonon spectral function (Sec.
V.5
).
To test for general validity, we perform additional calculations for n-doped MoS
2
; see Appendix
B
.

The trigonal-prismatic transition-metal dichalcogenide TaS
2
is long known to be a showplace of competing
CDW

Tidman et al. 1974
and superconductivity
Nagata et al. 1992
;
Vaňo et al. 2023
, which are suppressed and enhanced, respectively, when reducing the material thickness to the monolayer
Navarro-Moratalla et al. 2016
;
Yang et al. 2018
.
Based on
cDFPT
results
Berges et al. 2020
, the lattice instability and associated Kohn anomalies are exclusively due to low-energy electronic screening from an isolated half-filled band at the Fermi level.
These signs of significant electron-phonon coupling and well separable electronic energy scales make monolayer TaS
2
an ideal system to test the discussed methods and to study the different levels of electronic screening.

Figure 3:
(a–e) Electronic band structure of monolayer TaS
2
from
DFT
.
Possible choices for sets of active bands of increasing size are shown using thick mauve lines.
All colored bands (mauve or orange) have been Wannierized based on projections onto the indicated atomic orbitals.
(f–j) Corresponding phonon dispersions for a high Marzari-Vanderbilt smearing of
\sigma=20
mRy.
Screened and partially screened phonons from
DFPT
and
cDFPT
are shown using solid lines, renormalized (screened
cDFPT
and unscreened
DFPT
) phonons using dashed lines.

In the
ab initio
calculations, we apply the
PBE
(
PBE
) functional
Perdew et al. 1996
and corresponding norm-conserving pseudopotentials with nonlinear core correction and without
SC
(
SC
) states from the
PseudoDojo
table
Hamann 2013
;
van Setten et al. 2018
at an energy cutoff of 100 Ry.
We separate periodic images of the layer using a unit-cell height of 15 Å together with a truncation of the Coulomb interaction in this direction
Sohier et al. 2017
.
The relaxed lattice constant is
a=3.34
Å.
For the high-smearing starting point, we use a Marzari-Vanderbilt smearing
Marzari et al. 1999
of
\sigma=20
mRy in combination with uniform
12\times 12

\mathbf{k}
- and
\mathbf{q}
-point meshes (including
\Gamma
), sufficient for this smearing.
Reference low-temperature data are generated using a Fermi-Dirac smearing of
T=1.9
mRy (300 K) and
48\times 48

\mathbf{k}
points.
Minimizing forces to below 1 µRy/bohr yields a layer thickness (sulfur-sulfur distance) of
d=3.13
Å, which is recomputed for each considered smearing and
\mathbf{k}
mesh but does not change significantly.
For the Fourier interpolation, we use one-shot Wannier functions obtained from projections onto atomic orbitals (cf. Fig.
3
) to ensure perfect symmetry, with the exception of the 22-bands case where we use maximally localized Wannier functions (MLWF)
Marzari et al. 2012
with (i) a Ta-
2s
orbital, (ii) a
1s
orbital vertically centered between two S atoms, and (iii) another three
1s
orbitals halfway between (i) and (ii) as initial projections for the additional five conduction bands.

V.1
From screened to bare phonons

The Kohn-Sham band structure of monolayer TaS
2
from
DFT
is shown in Fig.
3
(a–e).
At the Fermi level, there is a half-filled isolated band of Ta-
d_{z^{2}}
, -
d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}
, and -
d_{xy}
orbital character.
These orbitals also span the two lower empty bands, which partially overlap but do not hybridize with the two higher empty bands of Ta-
d_{xz}
and -
d_{yz}
character.
The occupied bands are formed by four isolated blocks of (in order of decreasing energy) six S-
p
, two S-
s
, three Ta-
p
, and one Ta-
s
band.

To trace the transition from screened to bare phonons and interactions, we perform
cDFPT
calculations for active subspaces of different size, including zero (
DFPT
), one, five, 13, 17, and 22 bands.
Note that even if all bands explicitly calculated in
DFT
were considered active, the infinite number of empty bands accounted for via the Sternheimer approach as well as the core bands hidden into the pseudopotential would still contribute to the screening (cf. Appendix
C
).

The corresponding Fourier-interpolated phonon dispersions are shown in Fig.
3
(f–j).
The
DFPT
phonon dispersion, obtained from
D(\sigma,0)
via Eq. (
4
), is reproduced as a reference in each panel using solid orange lines.
It features a softening of the longitudinal-acoustic branch, most pronounced at
\mathbf{q}=2/3\,\mathrm{M}
, signaling the tendency toward the experimentally observed
3\times 3

CDW

Tidman et al. 1974
.
Interestingly, at the high Marzari-Vanderbilt smearing of
\sigma=20
mRy, the system is dynamically stable with no imaginary frequencies.

The partially screened phonons, obtained from
D^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
, corresponding to the different choices of active bands, are shown using solid mauve lines.
Excluding electronic screening from within the isolated band only [Fig.
3
(f)] already removes all
\mathbf{q}
-dependent softening of the longitudinal branch, which is now highest in energy among the acoustic branches.
For five active bands [Fig.
3
(g)], the situation is similar, except that the acoustic sum rule is no longer fulfilled because we freeze long-wavelength dipole-allowed transitions
van Loon et al. 2021a
, and the acoustic phonons acquire a finite energy at
\Gamma
(cf. Appendix
C
).
This effect is even more pronounced for 13 [Fig.
3
(h)], 17 [Fig.
3
(i)], and 22 [Fig.
3
(j)] active bands, where the partially screened dispersions have shifted to much higher energies, the originally acoustic phonons reaching about 175 meV in the latter case.
Note that some of the branches feature a finite slope near
\Gamma
, which is due to the lack of metallic screening and corresponds to long-range interactions in real space.
We used the electrostatic model introduced in Sec.
II.6.2
to properly interpolate these phonons, the details of which are found in the next section.

If we renormalize the partially screened phonons evaluating Eq. (
44
) at
\omega=0
using exactly the same smearing
T=\sigma
and
BZ
sampling as in the
ab initio
calculation, we obtain the dashed mauve lines in Fig.
3
(f–j).
They coincide with the
DFPT
result for all sizes of the active subspace, showing that the phonon self-energy with one partially screened and one screened vertex is exact as long as all involved quantities are exact too.
Note that all quantities entering Eq. (
44
) have been directly computed using
DFPT
and
cDFPT
on the coarse grid and only the resulting
D^{\textnormal{p0}}(\sigma,0)
has been Fourier interpolated along the high-symmetry lines shown in Fig.
3
(f–j).

Finally, in Fig.
3
(f–j) we also show the unscreened phonons from
D^{\textnormal{u}}(\sigma,0)
according to Eq. (
43
), again calculated on the original coarse mesh and interpolated only in the end, using dashed orange lines.
Since
g(\sigma,0)
is smaller than
g^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
,
\varPi^{\textnormal{00}}(\sigma,0)
is smaller than
\varPi^{\textnormal{p0}}(\sigma,0)
.
As a consequence, also the unscreened phonon frequencies are lower than (or at most equal to) the
cDFPT
ones; some
\mathbf{q}
-dependent softening is still present albeit hardly discernible because of the large smearing chosen.
A practical advantage is the absence of long-range terms
Pickett and Győrffy 1976
;
Allen 1980
; the slope of all branches vanishes toward
\Gamma
.

V.2
From screened to bare electron-phonon coupling

Figure 4:
Electron-phonon coupling of monolayer TaS
2
from (a) DFPT and (b–f) cDFPT for different sizes of the active subspace at a Marzari-Vanderbilt smearing of
\sigma=20
mRy.
We show the absolute value of the coupling to the isolated low-energy electronic band as a function of
\mathbf{q}
with
\mathbf{k}=0
for all phononic eigenmodes and using different approaches to handle the long-range part.
The black dots indicate direct
DFPT
and
cDFPT
results and serve as a reference.
The insets show the decay of the maximum absolute value of the short-range part (
\max\smash{|g_{\mathbf{R}}^{\mathcal{S}}|}
) with the length of the lattice vector
|\mathbf{R}|
[cf. Eq. (
61
)].

Now we will discuss the screened and partially screened electron-phonon coupling
g(\sigma,0)
and
g^{\textnormal{p}}(\sigma,0)
corresponding to the different active subspaces.
In Fig.
4
, the absolute value of the interpolated coupling with the isolated electronic band at the Fermi level is shown as a function of
\mathbf{q}
with
\mathbf{k}=0
for all nine phononic eigenmodes.
Reference data from direct
DFPT
and
cDFPT
calculations are shown using black dots.
Tick marks have been placed at those
\mathbf{q}
points that are part of the
12\times 12
mesh on which the interpolation is based.
The interpolated quantities are guaranteed to match the reference data at these original
\mathbf{q}
points by construction.

In the case of the screened
g(\sigma,0)
from
DFPT
shown in Fig.
4
(a), the interpolated coupling matches the reference coupling everywhere.
Here the system is metallic such that the coupling is continuous and smooth at the zone center, and due to the high electronic smearing there are no sharp features away from
\Gamma
either.
Thus, the coupling is short-ranged (inset).

However, as soon as low-energy screening is excluded, peaks at
\Gamma
emerge whose magnitude increases with the number of active bands, and the overall magnitude of the coupling increases too, as seen in Fig.
4
(b–f).
A naive Fourier interpolation of these data yields the gray curves, which by definition match the reference at all original
\mathbf{q}
points but are wrong in the vicinity of
\Gamma
.
This is because the peaks belong to the long-range part, which has to be subtracted before interpolation and added back afterward, as detailed in Sec.
II.6.2
.
The insets show the decay in real space with (gray) and without (mauve and orange) long-range part.

Using the equations in Sec.
II.6.2
with the dielectric constant
\epsilon
and Born effective charges
\mathbf{Z}^{*}
as obtained from the
cDFPT
calculation but neglecting the term in Eq. (
60
) that involves the quadrupole tensors
Q
, which cannot be calculated from perturbation theory with
Quantum ESPRESSO
at present, we obtain the orange lines.
Most of the reference points are reproduced, but there are still deviations for the two modes that peak when approaching
\Gamma
in Fig.
4
(b, c) as well as for the third mode with appreciable coupling for small
\mathbf{q}
in Fig.
4
(b–f).
The former discrepancy is likely due to inaccuracies in
\epsilon
or
\mathbf{Z}^{*}
.
The latter however occurs for a phonon mode where the atoms move in the out-of-plane direction and can be traced back to the missing term with
Q_{\text{S}z}
in Eq. (
60
).

The quadrupole tensors
Q
could be calculated
ab initio
with
Abinit

Gonze et al. 2016
;
Gonze et al. 2020
, albeit only within the local-density approximation and
PBE
exchange-correlation functionals and without nonlinear core correction
Royo and Stengel 2019
.

and fit the quadrupole tensors minimizing the error in the interpolated phonons and coupling for all reference
\mathbf{q}
points marked with black dots in Fig.

(b--f)
4
4

4

.

together with those of

and

from

and the optimal
L
are listed in Table

.
